People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, uh… so what brings me in is I’m having a hard time keeping my head on one thing. Like at work, I’ll start a safety briefing or we’ll be going over a plan review, and I’m trying to pay attention—I really am—but then a few minutes in my mind’s already jumped to three other job problems.

And then I miss stuff. Stuff I should’ve caught. Which, in construction, you know, that’s not really something you can just laugh off. I mean, there’s always a million things going on at a site, somebody asking about deliveries, somebody else needing a decision, and before I know it I’m not tracking the thing right in front of me. That’s been the big reason I’m here.

Like, about two years and a couple months.\n\nIt got a lot worse when the construction schedule changed and the hours got all chaotic. Before that I mean, I had days where I was scattered, sure, but once the schedule got turned upside down it just became… constant, or at least serious enough that it started causing real problems at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It feels like I’ve got to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Missed follow-ups, site coordination not being organized right—there’s been formal concern from my employer about it. I don’t know… I mean, anxious maybe? But I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly.

It’s more like there’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or remembering, and then I’m thinking about work, and then I’m thinking about stuff at home, and then I remember I never called somebody back—actually, that happened yesterday with a subcontractor, I had my phone in my hand and still didn’t do it.

But depressed… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling sad all the time or anything. It’s more frustration, I guess. Hard to separate it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. I mean, at home it’s the same kind of thing as work, honestly. Like if somebody gives me a few things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll get the first one or two done, and then something else grabs me, or someone calls, or I remember some totally different issue, and then later I find out I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed out the original problem.

And at home it’s like that with chores too—sorry, I know you asked about home—but it’s the same pattern. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.

I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.

It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / construction_manager / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Interrupting_Intruding\nmanifestation: \"I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking or someone working through paperwork and jump right in with my own point before I’ve been asked, and it rubs people the wrong way.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.

And sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”

Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.

I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… so what brings me in is I’m having a hard time keeping my head on one thing. Like at work, I’ll start a safety briefing or we’ll be going over a plan review, and I’m trying to pay attention—I really am—but then a few minutes in my mind’s already jumped to three other job problems.\n\nAnd then I miss stuff. Stuff I should’ve caught. Which, in construction, you know, that’s not really something you can just laugh off. I mean, there’s always a million things going on at a site, somebody asking about deliveries, somebody else needing a decision, and before I know it I’m not tracking the thing right in front of me. That’s been the big reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say a little over two years now where it’s really been messing with my job. Like, about two years and a couple months.\n\nIt got a lot worse when the construction schedule changed and the hours got all chaotic. Before that I mean, I had days where I was scattered, sure, but once the schedule got turned upside down it just became… constant, or at least serious enough that it started causing real problems at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is… hard. Like in meetings, when everybody else can just stay planted through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy getting up for coffee, or stepping outside to “check something,” or finding some excuse to walk around.\n\nSometimes I tell myself it’s work-related, like I need to go look at something on-site, but honestly a lot of the time I just can’t keep sitting there. It feels like I’ve got to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll cut somebody off because I think I already know what they’re about to ask, and I just jump in with the answer.\n\nLike at work someone starts explaining an issue, and before they finish I’m already talking over them, saying what we should do. Then halfway through I realize, oh, they hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. It’s embarrassing, and it can make things messy because I answered too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with my wife, I’d say. She’s pretty worn out from having to remind me about stuff I already said I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then it just… doesn’t happen, or I lose track of the follow-up.\n\nAnd I mean, that’s been happening at work too, which is probably part of why I’m more stressed at home. Missed follow-ups, site coordination not being organized right—there’s been formal concern from my employer about it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the guy keeping all the pieces moving.\n\nBut yeah, at home, it’s like my wife feels like she has to manage me too, and I can tell she’s tired of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, anxious maybe? But I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like there’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or remembering, and then I’m thinking about work, and then I’m thinking about stuff at home, and then I remember I never called somebody back—actually, that happened yesterday with a subcontractor, I had my phone in my hand and still didn’t do it.\n\nBut depressed… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling sad all the time or anything. It’s more frustration, I guess. Hard to separate it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your mind wandering or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, at home it’s the same kind of thing as work, honestly. Like if somebody gives me a few things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll get the first one or two done, and then something else grabs me, or someone calls, or I remember some totally different issue, and then later I find out I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed out the original problem.\n\nAnd at home it’s like that with chores too—sorry, I know you asked about home—but it’s the same pattern. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with friends, beyond what you've mentioned with your wife?\n\nPatient: With friends… not as much, I guess? I mean, I probably don’t keep up with people like I should, but the big problems are more at home and work.\n\nLike with my wife, she’s just tired of reminding me about things I already said I’d handle. And then work has been rough too—missed follow-ups, site coordination getting disorganized enough that my employer actually brought it up formally. That kind of stress follows me home, and then I’m not exactly fun to be around.\n\nBut friends specifically… I don’t think there’s been a big blow-up or anything. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. I’ll be driving home thinking about it, like, “How did I let that slip again?”

And then at home, my wife having to remind me over and over about stuff I said I’d handle… that feels awful. She’s worn down, and I can see it. It’s like—well, my dad used to say if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, and I believe that, but somehow I still drop the ball. So yeah, it definitely affects how I feel about myself and how things are going overall.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… so what brings me in is I’m having a hard time keeping my head on one thing. Like at work, I’ll start a safety briefing or we’ll be going over a plan review, and I’m trying to pay attention—I really am—but then a few minutes in my mind’s already jumped to three other job problems.\n\nAnd then I miss stuff. Stuff I should’ve caught. Which, in construction, you know, that’s not really something you can just laugh off. I mean, there’s always a million things going on at a site, somebody asking about deliveries, somebody else needing a decision, and before I know it I’m not tracking the thing right in front of me. That’s been the big reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say a little over two years now where it’s really been messing with my job. Like, about two years and a couple months.\n\nIt got a lot worse when the construction schedule changed and the hours got all chaotic. Before that I mean, I had days where I was scattered, sure, but once the schedule got turned upside down it just became… constant, or at least serious enough that it started causing real problems at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is… hard. Like in meetings, when everybody else can just stay planted through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy getting up for coffee, or stepping outside to “check something,” or finding some excuse to walk around.\n\nSometimes I tell myself it’s work-related, like I need to go look at something on-site, but honestly a lot of the time I just can’t keep sitting there. It feels like I’ve got to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll cut somebody off because I think I already know what they’re about to ask, and I just jump in with the answer.\n\nLike at work someone starts explaining an issue, and before they finish I’m already talking over them, saying what we should do. Then halfway through I realize, oh, they hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. It’s embarrassing, and it can make things messy because I answered too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with my wife, I’d say. She’s pretty worn out from having to remind me about stuff I already said I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then it just… doesn’t happen, or I lose track of the follow-up.\n\nAnd I mean, that’s been happening at work too, which is probably part of why I’m more stressed at home. Missed follow-ups, site coordination not being organized right—there’s been formal concern from my employer about it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the guy keeping all the pieces moving.\n\nBut yeah, at home, it’s like my wife feels like she has to manage me too, and I can tell she’s tired of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, anxious maybe? But I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like there’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or remembering, and then I’m thinking about work, and then I’m thinking about stuff at home, and then I remember I never called somebody back—actually, that happened yesterday with a subcontractor, I had my phone in my hand and still didn’t do it.\n\nBut depressed… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling sad all the time or anything. It’s more frustration, I guess. Hard to separate it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your mind wandering or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, at home it’s the same kind of thing as work, honestly. Like if somebody gives me a few things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll get the first one or two done, and then something else grabs me, or someone calls, or I remember some totally different issue, and then later I find out I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed out the original problem.\n\nAnd at home it’s like that with chores too—sorry, I know you asked about home—but it’s the same pattern. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with friends, beyond what you've mentioned with your wife?\n\nPatient: With friends… not as much, I guess? I mean, I probably don’t keep up with people like I should, but the big problems are more at home and work.\n\nLike with my wife, she’s just tired of reminding me about things I already said I’d handle. And then work has been rough too—missed follow-ups, site coordination getting disorganized enough that my employer actually brought it up formally. That kind of stress follows me home, and then I’m not exactly fun to be around.\n\nBut friends specifically… I don’t think there’s been a big blow-up or anything. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. I remember those notes coming home and—actually, my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d swear I had the homework in my backpack and then somehow it was just gone. But yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life, beyond just the issues at work and with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with friends, beyond what you've mentioned with your wife?\n\nPatient: With friends… not as much, I guess? I mean, I probably don’t keep up with people like I should, but the big problems are more at home and work.\n\nLike with my wife, she’s just tired of reminding me about things I already said I’d handle. And then work has been rough too—missed follow-ups, site coordination getting disorganized enough that my employer actually brought it up formally. That kind of stress follows me home, and then I’m not exactly fun to be around.\n\nBut friends specifically… I don’t think there’s been a big blow-up or anything. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. I remember those notes coming home and—actually, my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d swear I had the homework in my backpack and then somehow it was just gone. But yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life, beyond just the issues at work and with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Like I’ll fully mean to do it, and then—actually this happened with something around the house the other day, I can’t remember if it was a bill or a repair thing—but she ends up carrying the mental list, and I can tell she’s tired of being in that role. So yeah, it’s not just a little annoyance. It’s affecting day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties have caused you a lot of distress or significantly impacted your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. I’ll be driving home thinking about it, like, “How did I let that slip again?”\n\nAnd then at home, my wife having to remind me over and over about stuff I said I’d handle… that feels awful. Yeah, I think so. I mean, work and home are the obvious ones now, but it wasn’t like this just started.

Back in elementary school, teachers were already talking to my parents about me not staying in my seat, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing homework… stuff like that. I’d also rush through directions before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Which, honestly, I remember because my mom would get those notes from school and then there’d be this whole conversation at home—well, not a conversation exactly, more like me being told I needed to “slow down and listen.”

So yeah, it’s been there in school when I was younger, and now it shows up at work and at home too.

Like at work, I’ll start a safety briefing or we’ll be going over a plan review, and I’m trying to pay attention—I really am—but then a few minutes in my mind’s already jumped to three other job problems.\n\nAnd then I miss stuff. Stuff I should’ve caught. Which, in construction, you know, that’s not really something you can just laugh off. I mean, there’s always a million things going on at a site, somebody asking about deliveries, somebody else needing a decision, and before I know it I’m not tracking the thing right in front of me. That’s been the big reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say a little over two years now where it’s really been messing with my job. Like, about two years and a couple months.\n\nIt got a lot worse when the construction schedule changed and the hours got all chaotic. Before that I mean, I had days where I was scattered, sure, but once the schedule got turned upside down it just became… constant, or at least serious enough that it started causing real problems at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is… hard. Like in meetings, when everybody else can just stay planted through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy getting up for coffee, or stepping outside to “check something,” or finding some excuse to walk around.\n\nSometimes I tell myself it’s work-related, like I need to go look at something on-site, but honestly a lot of the time I just can’t keep sitting there. It feels like I’ve got to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll cut somebody off because I think I already know what they’re about to ask, and I just jump in with the answer.\n\nLike at work someone starts explaining an issue, and before they finish I’m already talking over them, saying what we should do. Then halfway through I realize, oh, they hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. It’s embarrassing, and it can make things messy because I answered too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with my wife, I’d say. She’s pretty worn out from having to remind me about stuff I already said I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then it just… doesn’t happen, or I lose track of the follow-up.\n\nAnd I mean, that’s been happening at work too, which is probably part of why I’m more stressed at home. Missed follow-ups, site coordination not being organized right—there’s been formal concern from my employer about it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the guy keeping all the pieces moving.\n\nBut yeah, at home, it’s like my wife feels like she has to manage me too, and I can tell she’s tired of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, anxious maybe? But I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like there’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or remembering, and then I’m thinking about work, and then I’m thinking about stuff at home, and then I remember I never called somebody back—actually, that happened yesterday with a subcontractor, I had my phone in my hand and still didn’t do it.\n\nBut depressed… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling sad all the time or anything. It’s more frustration, I guess. Hard to separate it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your mind wandering or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, at home it’s the same kind of thing as work, honestly. Like if somebody gives me a few things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll get the first one or two done, and then something else grabs me, or someone calls, or I remember some totally different issue, and then later I find out I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed out the original problem.\n\nAnd at home it’s like that with chores too—sorry, I know you asked about home—but it’s the same pattern. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with friends, beyond what you've mentioned with your wife?\n\nPatient: With friends… not as much, I guess? I mean, I probably don’t keep up with people like I should, but the big problems are more at home and work.\n\nLike with my wife, she’s just tired of reminding me about things I already said I’d handle. And then work has been rough too—missed follow-ups, site coordination getting disorganized enough that my employer actually brought it up formally. That kind of stress follows me home, and then I’m not exactly fun to be around.\n\nBut friends specifically… I don’t think there’s been a big blow-up or anything. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. I remember those notes coming home and—actually, my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d swear I had the homework in my backpack and then somehow it was just gone. But yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life, beyond just the issues at work and with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Like I’ll fully mean to do it, and then—actually this happened with something around the house the other day, I can’t remember if it was a bill or a repair thing—but she ends up carrying the mental list, and I can tell she’s tired of being in that role. So yeah, it’s not just a little annoyance. It’s affecting day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties have caused you a lot of distress or significantly impacted your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. I’ll be driving home thinking about it, like, “How did I let that slip again?”\n\nAnd then at home, my wife having to remind me over and over about stuff I said I’d handle… that feels awful. She’s worn down, and I can see it. It’s like—well, my dad used to say if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, and I believe that, but somehow I still drop the ball. So yeah, it definitely affects how I feel about myself and how things are going overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties at home, at work, and in other settings, like when you're out with friends or in public places?\n\nPatient: I mean… home and work, definitely, yeah, those are the big ones I notice because that’s where the consequences show up.\n\nWith friends or out in public… I don’t know, it’s harder for me to say. Like I’m not usually keeping score in the grocery store or whatever. Sometimes I’ll be talking and then remember something totally unrelated—like I’ll be in the middle of a conversation and think about a tool order or whether I locked the truck, and then I’ve kind of lost the thread—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, yeah, I can say home and work for sure. Other places, I’m not as sure how much it shows.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in most areas of your life, including school when you were younger, and now at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, work and home are the obvious ones now, but it wasn’t like this just started.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already talking to my parents about me not staying in my seat, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing homework… stuff like that. I’d also rush through directions before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Which, honestly, I remember because my mom would get those notes from school and then there’d be this whole conversation at home—well, not a conversation exactly, more like me being told I needed to “slow down and listen.”\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there in school when I was younger, and now it shows up at work and at home too.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So yeah, it definitely affects how I feel about myself and how things are going overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties at home, at work, and in other settings, like when you're out with friends or in public places?\n\nPatient: I mean… home and work, definitely, yeah, those are the big ones I notice because that’s where the consequences show up.\n\nWith friends or out in public… I don’t know, it’s harder for me to say. Like I’m not usually keeping score in the grocery store or whatever. Sometimes I’ll be talking and then remember something totally unrelated—like I’ll be in the middle of a conversation and think about a tool order or whether I locked the truck, and then I’ve kind of lost the thread—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, yeah, I can say home and work for sure. Other places, I’m not as sure how much it shows.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in most areas of your life, including school when you were younger, and now at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, work and home are the obvious ones now, but it wasn’t like this just started.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already talking to my parents about me not staying in my seat, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing homework… stuff like that. I’d also rush through directions before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Which, honestly, I remember because my mom would get those notes from school and then there’d be this whole conversation at home—well, not a conversation exactly, more like me being told I needed to “slow down and listen.”\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there in school when I was younger, and now it shows up at work and at home too.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of several months or more where you didn't experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Not really, no. I mean, it’s varied some, but I can’t think of a stretch for several months where it just wasn’t an issue.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… so what brings me in is I’m having a hard time keeping my head on one thing. Like at work, I’ll start a safety briefing or we’ll be going over a plan review, and I’m trying to pay attention—I really am—but then a few minutes in my mind’s already jumped to three other job problems.\n\nAnd then I miss stuff. Stuff I should’ve caught. Which, in construction, you know, that’s not really something you can just laugh off. I mean, there’s always a million things going on at a site, somebody asking about deliveries, somebody else needing a decision, and before I know it I’m not tracking the thing right in front of me. That’s been the big reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say a little over two years now where it’s really been messing with my job. Like, about two years and a couple months.\n\nIt got a lot worse when the construction schedule changed and the hours got all chaotic. Before that I mean, I had days where I was scattered, sure, but once the schedule got turned upside down it just became… constant, or at least serious enough that it started causing real problems at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is… hard. Like in meetings, when everybody else can just stay planted through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy getting up for coffee, or stepping outside to “check something,” or finding some excuse to walk around.\n\nSometimes I tell myself it’s work-related, like I need to go look at something on-site, but honestly a lot of the time I just can’t keep sitting there. It feels like I’ve got to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll cut somebody off because I think I already know what they’re about to ask, and I just jump in with the answer.\n\nLike at work someone starts explaining an issue, and before they finish I’m already talking over them, saying what we should do. Then halfway through I realize, oh, they hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. It’s embarrassing, and it can make things messy because I answered too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with my wife, I’d say. She’s pretty worn out from having to remind me about stuff I already said I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then it just… doesn’t happen, or I lose track of the follow-up.\n\nAnd I mean, that’s been happening at work too, which is probably part of why I’m more stressed at home. Missed follow-ups, site coordination not being organized right—there’s been formal concern from my employer about it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the guy keeping all the pieces moving.\n\nBut yeah, at home, it’s like my wife feels like she has to manage me too, and I can tell she’s tired of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, anxious maybe? But I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like there’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or remembering, and then I’m thinking about work, and then I’m thinking about stuff at home, and then I remember I never called somebody back—actually, that happened yesterday with a subcontractor, I had my phone in my hand and still didn’t do it.\n\nBut depressed… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling sad all the time or anything. It’s more frustration, I guess. Hard to separate it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your mind wandering or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, at home it’s the same kind of thing as work, honestly. Like if somebody gives me a few things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll get the first one or two done, and then something else grabs me, or someone calls, or I remember some totally different issue, and then later I find out I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed out the original problem.\n\nAnd at home it’s like that with chores too—sorry, I know you asked about home—but it’s the same pattern. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with friends, beyond what you've mentioned with your wife?\n\nPatient: With friends… not as much, I guess? I mean, I probably don’t keep up with people like I should, but the big problems are more at home and work.\n\nLike with my wife, she’s just tired of reminding me about things I already said I’d handle. And then work has been rough too—missed follow-ups, site coordination getting disorganized enough that my employer actually brought it up formally. That kind of stress follows me home, and then I’m not exactly fun to be around.\n\nBut friends specifically… I don’t think there’s been a big blow-up or anything. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. I remember those notes coming home and—actually, my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d swear I had the homework in my backpack and then somehow it was just gone. But yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life, beyond just the issues at work and with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Like I’ll fully mean to do it, and then—actually this happened with something around the house the other day, I can’t remember if it was a bill or a repair thing—but she ends up carrying the mental list, and I can tell she’s tired of being in that role. So yeah, it’s not just a little annoyance. It’s affecting day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties have caused you a lot of distress or significantly impacted your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. I remember those notes coming home and—actually, my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d swear I had the homework in my backpack and then somehow it was just gone. But yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life, beyond just the issues at work and with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Like I’ll fully mean to do it, and then—actually this happened with something around the house the other day, I can’t remember if it was a bill or a repair thing—but she ends up carrying the mental list, and I can tell she’s tired of being in that role. So yeah, it’s not just a little annoyance. It’s affecting day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties have caused you a lot of distress or significantly impacted your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. I’ll be driving home thinking about it, like, “How did I let that slip again?”\n\nAnd then at home, my wife having to remind me over and over about stuff I said I’d handle… that feels awful. She’s worn down, and I can see it. It’s like—well, my dad used to say if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, and I believe that, but somehow I still drop the ball. So yeah, it definitely affects how I feel about myself and how things are going overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties at home, at work, and in other settings, like when you're out with friends or in public places?\n\nPatient: I mean… home and work, definitely, yeah, those are the big ones I notice because that’s where the consequences show up.\n\nWith friends or out in public… I don’t know, it’s harder for me to say. Like I’m not usually keeping score in the grocery store or whatever. Sometimes I’ll be talking and then remember something totally unrelated—like I’ll be in the middle of a conversation and think about a tool order or whether I locked the truck, and then I’ve kind of lost the thread—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, yeah, I can say home and work for sure. Other places, I’m not as sure how much it shows.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in most areas of your life, including school when you were younger, and now at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, work and home are the obvious ones now, but it wasn’t like this just started.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already talking to my parents about me not staying in my seat, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing homework… stuff like that. I’d also rush through directions before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Which, honestly, I remember because my mom would get those notes from school and then there’d be this whole conversation at home—well, not a conversation exactly, more like me being told I needed to “slow down and listen.”\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there in school when I was younger, and now it shows up at work and at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several months or more where you didn't experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s varied some, but I can’t think of a stretch for several months where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nThe job part has gotten seriously worse for about… two years and a couple months now, since the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic. Before that I could kind of keep a rhythm better, or at least fake it better. Once the hours started shifting around, everything got harder to keep straight.\n\nSorry, I’m probably drifting from what you asked. But no, I don’t think there’s been a long period where it went away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Like I’ll fully mean to do it, and then—actually this happened with something around the house the other day, I can’t remember if it was a bill or a repair thing—but she ends up carrying the mental list, and I can tell she’s tired of being in that role. So yeah, it’s not just a little annoyance. It’s affecting day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties have caused you a lot of distress or significantly impacted your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. I’ll be driving home thinking about it, like, “How did I let that slip again?”\n\nAnd then at home, my wife having to remind me over and over about stuff I said I’d handle… that feels awful. She’s worn down, and I can see it. It’s like—well, my dad used to say if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, and I believe that, but somehow I still drop the ball. Other places, I’m not as sure how much it shows.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in most areas of your life, including school when you were younger, and now at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ve had a few different jobs, and I don’t remember each one well enough to say “yes, every single one.”

I mean, construction is the one I think about because that’s my life now, and it’s where the problems are obvious. Earlier jobs… I know I had moments where things were harder for me than they seemed to be for other people, but I don’t want to pretend I’ve got a perfect timeline in my head.

So, I guess I’d say it probably wasn’t only this current job, but I can’t give you a definite answer for every job I’ve had.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… so what brings me in is I’m having a hard time keeping my head on one thing. Like at work, I’ll start a safety briefing or we’ll be going over a plan review, and I’m trying to pay attention—I really am—but then a few minutes in my mind’s already jumped to three other job problems.\n\nAnd then I miss stuff. Stuff I should’ve caught. Which, in construction, you know, that’s not really something you can just laugh off. I mean, there’s always a million things going on at a site, somebody asking about deliveries, somebody else needing a decision, and before I know it I’m not tracking the thing right in front of me. That’s been the big reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having trouble keeping your mind on one thing?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say a little over two years now where it’s really been messing with my job. Like, about two years and a couple months.\n\nIt got a lot worse when the construction schedule changed and the hours got all chaotic. Before that I mean, I had days where I was scattered, sure, but once the schedule got turned upside down it just became… constant, or at least serious enough that it started causing real problems at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is… hard. Like in meetings, when everybody else can just stay planted through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy getting up for coffee, or stepping outside to “check something,” or finding some excuse to walk around.\n\nSometimes I tell myself it’s work-related, like I need to go look at something on-site, but honestly a lot of the time I just can’t keep sitting there. It feels like I’ve got to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. A lot of times I’ll cut somebody off because I think I already know what they’re about to ask, and I just jump in with the answer.\n\nLike at work someone starts explaining an issue, and before they finish I’m already talking over them, saying what we should do. Then halfway through I realize, oh, they hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. It’s embarrassing, and it can make things messy because I answered too fast.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly with my wife, I’d say. She’s pretty worn out from having to remind me about stuff I already said I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it,” and then it just… doesn’t happen, or I lose track of the follow-up.\n\nAnd I mean, that’s been happening at work too, which is probably part of why I’m more stressed at home. Missed follow-ups, site coordination not being organized right—there’s been formal concern from my employer about it. Which is embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the guy keeping all the pieces moving.\n\nBut yeah, at home, it’s like my wife feels like she has to manage me too, and I can tell she’s tired of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, anxious maybe? But I don’t know if I’d call it that exactly.\n\nIt’s more like there’s always something I’m supposed to be doing or remembering, and then I’m thinking about work, and then I’m thinking about stuff at home, and then I remember I never called somebody back—actually, that happened yesterday with a subcontractor, I had my phone in my hand and still didn’t do it.\n\nBut depressed… I’m not sure. I wouldn’t say I’m walking around feeling sad all the time or anything. It’s more frustration, I guess. Hard to separate it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble completing tasks or chores at home due to your mind wandering or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. I mean, at home it’s the same kind of thing as work, honestly. Like if somebody gives me a few things to follow up on after a site walk, I’ll get the first one or two done, and then something else grabs me, or someone calls, or I remember some totally different issue, and then later I find out I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed out the original problem.\n\nAnd at home it’s like that with chores too—sorry, I know you asked about home—but it’s the same pattern. I start something, then my mind goes somewhere else, and the thing just doesn’t get finished. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to blow it off. It just kind of falls out of my head once something else comes up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships with friends, beyond what you've mentioned with your wife?\n\nPatient: With friends… not as much, I guess? I mean, I probably don’t keep up with people like I should, but the big problems are more at home and work.\n\nLike with my wife, she’s just tired of reminding me about things I already said I’d handle. And then work has been rough too—missed follow-ups, site coordination getting disorganized enough that my employer actually brought it up formally. That kind of stress follows me home, and then I’m not exactly fun to be around.\n\nBut friends specifically… I don’t think there’s been a big blow-up or anything. It’s more the people closest to me getting the worst of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I get wrapped up in things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d say I lose track of everything around me.\n\nLike, if I’m looking at a plan or trying to figure out a schedule issue, I might stay on it for a bit—though then somebody walks in or my phone goes off and I’m onto the next thing. Actually that reminds me, the phones on job sites are constant, it’s like you can’t get five minutes sometimes.\n\nBut yeah… so, to your question, I don’t really know that I’ve noticed that kind of tunnel-vision thing. Not in a big way, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, looking back, yeah. Even in elementary school my teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d talk before I was supposed to. I remember stuff like homework disappearing or not getting turned in, and I’d rush through directions before I actually understood what they wanted.\n\nIt’s funny, I hadn’t thought about some of that in a while—my parents used to get those notes from school, and then it would be this whole thing at home, and I’d be like, “I didn’t mean to.” But yeah, it was there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity in social situations, like when you're with friends or in public?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess that does happen. Especially on job sites, which is kind of social and work mixed together, you know? Like I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking, or someone’s in the middle of paperwork, and I’ll just jump in with whatever point I had in my head before anyone’s really asked me.\n\nAnd sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I see their face, like I cut them off or came in too strong. It’s not like I’m trying to be rude. I just have the thought and it feels like I need to say it right then, and then later I’m thinking, “Yeah, that probably rubbed them the wrong way.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity pretty consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. I mean, if I think back, it wasn’t like this just started when I got older or started working construction.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, I’d talk out of turn, lose homework, rush through directions before I really understood what they wanted. I remember those notes coming home and—actually, my mom used to get so frustrated because she’d swear I had the homework in my backpack and then somehow it was just gone. But yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life, beyond just the issues at work and with your wife?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s hard to separate it out, because work and home are most of my daily life, you know? But yeah, it’s significant. Like at work it got to the point where missed follow-ups and me not keeping the site coordination organized enough turned into formal concern from my employer, which was… not great.\n\nAnd at home, my wife is just worn out from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. Like I’ll fully mean to do it, and then—actually this happened with something around the house the other day, I can’t remember if it was a bill or a repair thing—but she ends up carrying the mental list, and I can tell she’s tired of being in that role. So yeah, it’s not just a little annoyance. It’s affecting day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your difficulties have caused you a lot of distress or significantly impacted your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been pretty distressing. I try not to make a huge deal out of it, but when your boss is formally concerned because you missed follow-ups or the site coordination isn’t organized right, that sits with you. I’ll be driving home thinking about it, like, “How did I let that slip again?”\n\nAnd then at home, my wife having to remind me over and over about stuff I said I’d handle… that feels awful. She’s worn down, and I can see it. It’s like—well, my dad used to say if you say you’re gonna do something, you do it, and I believe that, but somehow I still drop the ball. So yeah, it definitely affects how I feel about myself and how things are going overall.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties at home, at work, and in other settings, like when you're out with friends or in public places?\n\nPatient: I mean… home and work, definitely, yeah, those are the big ones I notice because that’s where the consequences show up.\n\nWith friends or out in public… I don’t know, it’s harder for me to say. Like I’m not usually keeping score in the grocery store or whatever. Sometimes I’ll be talking and then remember something totally unrelated—like I’ll be in the middle of a conversation and think about a tool order or whether I locked the truck, and then I’ve kind of lost the thread—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo, yeah, I can say home and work for sure. Other places, I’m not as sure how much it shows.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally experienced these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in most areas of your life, including school when you were younger, and now at work and at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, work and home are the obvious ones now, but it wasn’t like this just started.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already talking to my parents about me not staying in my seat, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing homework… stuff like that. I’d also rush through directions before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Which, honestly, I remember because my mom would get those notes from school and then there’d be this whole conversation at home—well, not a conversation exactly, more like me being told I needed to “slow down and listen.”\n\nSo yeah, it’s been there in school when I was younger, and now it shows up at work and at home too.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several months or more where you didn't experience these difficulties with attention and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, it’s varied some, but I can’t think of a stretch for several months where it just wasn’t an issue.\n\nThe job part has gotten seriously worse for about… two years and a couple months now, since the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic. Before that I could kind of keep a rhythm better, or at least fake it better. Once the hours started shifting around, everything got harder to keep straight.\n\nSorry, I’m probably drifting from what you asked. But no, I don’t think there’s been a long period where it went away.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time in your life, even if just for a little while, where you felt like you didn't have these problems with attention and impulsivity at all?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really think of a time where it was just gone completely. I mean, maybe there were times where life was more predictable and I handled it better, but the problems were still kind of there.\n\nThe big change was about two years and two months ago, when the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic. Since then it’s been seriously messing with my job. Before that I might’ve looked more put together, but I wouldn’t say I didn’t have the problems at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally had these difficulties with attention and impulsivity in every job you've had, not just your current one in construction?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I’ve had a few different jobs, and I don’t remember each one well enough to say “yes, every single one.”\n\nI mean, construction is the one I think about because that’s my life now, and it’s where the problems are obvious. Earlier jobs… I know I had moments where things were harder for me than they seemed to be for other people, but I don’t want to pretend I’ve got a perfect timeline in my head.\n\nSo, I guess I’d say it probably wasn’t only this current job, but I can’t give you a definite answer for every job I’ve had.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
